```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
5	
6	\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}
7	\label{sec:validation}
8	
9	A power analysis using empirical MMLU IRT parameters confirms that at $N \geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$. Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold.
10	
11	\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\% Item Instability}
12	\label{sec:temporal}
13	
14	Comparing 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models, 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\% $\to$ 31.30\%), because the dual ETS threshold on effect size and significance already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.
15	
16	Three controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\%.
17	
18	Within-subject analysis yields an average of 38.1\% ETS~C items across all five MMLU domains (range: 31--45\%). Despite an ecological correlation of $\rho = 0.307$ ($p = 0.02$) between subject-level C\% and accuracy improvement, item-level logistic regression reveals that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\% C\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$; counterfactual C\% $= 29.0\%$). Thus 92.4\% of the instability reflects item-specific changes unrelated to aggregate domain-level gains. The qualitative conclusion is robust across ETS thresholds: C\% $= 48.9\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.0$, $18.3\%$ at $\geq 2.0$, and $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$. \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape} summarizes the DIF landscape across all cohort comparisons.
19	
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$. FPR is measured against Li et al.\ web-overlap labels on the ``clean'' (non-overlapping) subset. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels.}
23	\label{tab:temporal_landscape}
24	\small
25	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
26	\toprule
27	Cohort Definition & $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\mathrm{focal}}$ & ETS~C (\%) & FPR (Li clean) & Enrichment \\
28	\midrule
29	Temporal (2023 vs.\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.96 \\
30	Ability quintile (Q1--2 vs.\ Q4--5) & ${\sim}$2,600 / ${\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\
31	Within-family (Llama-2 vs.\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\
32	Random split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\
33	Within-subject (avg, 5 subjects) & \textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\
34	\bottomrule
35	\end{tabular}
36	\end{table}
37	
38	\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}
39	\label{sec:groundtruth}
40	
41	The enrichment ratio of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} is 0.96 (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}), indistinguishable from chance. This null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$, ${+}0.674$ at $p = 1.0$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). An item can be globally contaminated yet function identically if all cohorts have comparable access. The Li et al.\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method: the near-chance enrichment is expected, not evidence of failure.
42	
43	\begin{figure}[t]
44	\centering
45	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
46	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
47	\label{fig:dose_response}
48	\end{figure}
49	
50	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
51	\label{sec:structural}
52	
53	Three progressively refined approaches (matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\theta$-conditioning, within-subject DIF) all fail to reduce the temporal FPR below 0.31. $\theta$-conditioning achieves $r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$ yet FPR $= 0.312$, and within-subject C\% $= 38.1\%$ exceeds the 25\% bifactor viability threshold. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\% of pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\ 2.2\% cross-subject; $p = 2.3 \times 10^{-9}$), but cross-subject matching addresses this bias and temporal C\% persists. \Cref{app:structural} reports full results.
54	
55	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}
56	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
57	
58	The critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\Cref{app:fig_matching}).
59	
60	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
61	\label{sec:downstream}
62	
63	Spearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\rho \approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Phi-3 and Llama-3 gain ${+}700$ to ${+}850$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based merges lose $-400$ to $-587$ positions (\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). The DIF instability pattern extends beyond MMLU; \Cref{sec:gsm8k} presents a full replication on GSM8K.
64	
65	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}
66	\label{sec:gsm8k}
67	
68	Replication on GSM8K (1,319 items $\times$ 6,702 models) yields temporal C\% $= 18.9\%$ (sanity check: 0.3\%), with semi-synthetic $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.818$ at $p = 0.7$ (\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). The GSM1K held-out set~\citep{zhang2024gsm1k} provides independent contamination validation: for $N = 33$ models, the correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage is $r = -0.478$ ($p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$ after controlling for accuracy). This \emph{negative} correlation contradicts the hypothesis that DIF items benefit contaminated models, and instead suggests that DIF captures genuine capability change distinct from memorization (\Cref{fig:gsm1k}). The result is suggestive at $N = 33$ and warrants replication.
69	
70	\begin{figure}[t]
71	\centering
72	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}
73	\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}
74	\label{fig:gsm1k}
75	\end{figure}
76

Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\\% $\\to$ 31.30\\%), because the dual ETS threshold on effect size and significance already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.\n\nThree controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps that confound the DIF signal~\\citep{holland1988mh}. The contrast between 2.9\\% (ability-controlled) and 61.3\\% (ability-confounded) brackets the temporal rate of 31.3\\%.\n\nWithin-subject analysis yields an average of 38.1\\% ETS~C items across all five MMLU domains (range: 31--45\\%). Despite an ecological correlation of $\\rho = 0.307$ ($p = 0.02$) between subject-level C\\% and accuracy improvement, item-level logistic regression reveals that domain accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\\% C\\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$; counterfactual C\\% $= 29.0\\%$). Thus 92.4\\% of the instability reflects item-specific changes unrelated to aggregate domain-level gains. The qualitative conclusion is robust across ETS thresholds: C\\% $= 48.9\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.0$, $18.3\\%$ at $\\geq 2.0$, and $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$. \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape} summarizes the DIF landscape across all cohort comparisons.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. FPR is measured against Li et al.\\ web-overlap labels on the ``clean'' (non-overlapping) subset. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & ETS~C (\\%) & FPR (Li clean) & Enrichment \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.96 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subjects) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\nThe enrichment ratio of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} is 0.96 (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}), indistinguishable from chance. This null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$, ${+}0.674$ at $p = 1.0$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). An item can be globally contaminated yet function identically if all cohorts have comparable access. The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method: the near-chance enrichment is expected, not evidence of failure.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF detects differential contamination when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\nThree progressively refined approaches (matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, within-subject DIF) all fail to reduce the temporal FPR below 0.31. $\\theta$-conditioning achieves $r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$ yet FPR $= 0.312$, and within-subject C\\% $= 38.1\\%$ exceeds the 25\\% bifactor viability threshold. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\% of pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\ 2.2\\% cross-subject; $p = 2.3 \\times 10^{-9}$), but cross-subject matching addresses this bias and temporal C\\% persists. \\Cref{app:structural} reports full results.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\nThe critical methodological finding is that the choice of matching strategy determines validity. Cross-subject matching computes the conditioning variable from subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested, achieving $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects while four of five produce $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$. Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average because the matching variable absorbs part of the injection signal. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol (\\Cref{app:fig_matching}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\nSpearman correlation between full and stable-item rankings is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$: aggregate model ordering is preserved despite 31\\% item instability. DIF-flagged items nonetheless carry more discriminative signal than average ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal across 21 conditions), though a circularity ablation shows DIF-based removal is the \\emph{least} disruptive among non-random strategies (Jaccard overlap with high-discrimination items is only 0.12). Individual model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Phi-3 and Llama-3 gain ${+}700$ to ${+}850$ rank positions under stable-item evaluation, while Mistral-based merges lose $-400$ to $-587$ positions (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). The DIF instability pattern extends beyond MMLU; \\Cref{sec:gsm8k} presents a full replication on GSM8K.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\nReplication on GSM8K (1,319 items $\\times$ 6,702 models) yields temporal C\\% $= 18.9\\%$ (sanity check: 0.3\\%), with semi-synthetic $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.818$ at $p = 0.7$ (\\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). The GSM1K held-out set~\\citep{zhang2024gsm1k} provides independent contamination validation: for $N = 33$ models, the correlation between contamination gap and DIF ranking advantage is $r = -0.478$ ($p = 0.005$; partial $r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$ after controlling for accuracy). This \\emph{negative} correlation contradicts the hypothesis that DIF items benefit contaminated models, and instead suggests that DIF captures genuine capability change distinct from memorization (\\Cref{fig:gsm1k}). The result is suggestive at $N = 33$ and warrants replication.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items.}\n\\label{fig:gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n",

1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}
5	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
6	
7	The 31\% ETS~C instability rate is robust across thresholds (10--49\% at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \in [2.5, 1.0]$) and confirmed by three structural controls (\Cref{sec:structural}). Domain-level accuracy improvement explains only 2.4 percentage points of 31.3\% C\% (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); 92.4\% of the instability is item-specific and unrelated to aggregate capability gains. This instability does not invalidate MMLU as a ranking instrument ($\rho \approx 0.997$ between full and stable-item rankings) but signals that item-level measurement properties are drifting across model generations. Benchmark designers should monitor DIF as a quality diagnostic: an item pool where C\% grows over successive cohorts indicates measurement drift even when rank ordering remains intact.
8	
9	\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}
10	\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}
11	
12	The near-chance enrichment (0.96) between DIF flags and web-overlap labels exposes a gap that extends beyond our analysis: all prior work validating item-level contamination detectors against web-overlap or $n$-gram labels conflates global exposure with differential functioning. Most MMLU items appear in web text, so web-overlap labels identify a condition shared by nearly all items rather than one that differentiates model cohorts. The enrichment of $\approx 1.0$ is the expected outcome when a differential method is evaluated against a global-exposure reference, not evidence of failure. The field needs validation methodologies that match the detection granularity: item-level differential detectors require item-level differential ground truth.
13	
14	\subsection{DIF as Diagnostic Lens}
15	\label{sec:disc_diagnostic}
16	
17	The GSM1K correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \Cref{sec:gsm8k}) provides evidence that DIF-flagged items capture behavioral change distinct from memorization. Models with larger contamination gaps benefit \emph{less} from DIF-C items; the negative correlation concentrates in the low-discrimination subset (171 of 249 focal-favoring DIF-C items have $\alpha < 1$). Partial correlation ($r = -0.385$, $p = 0.027$) after controlling for accuracy confirms the effect is not an artifact of general model quality. Caveats include a wide confidence interval (95\% CI $= [-0.71, -0.16]$) and limited sample size.
18	
19	Three structural constraints limit DIF applicability in LLM benchmarks: (1) MMLU's multi-domain structure produces mild local dependence that elevates within-subject DIF rates (\Cref{sec:structural}); (2) reliable detection requires $N \geq 80$ per cohort and $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$; and (3) ability differences between temporal cohorts confound the DIF signal (within-family C\% $= 61.3\%$). Higher-level methods (MIMIC models, propensity-score matching, multidimensional IRT) may address these constraints.
20	
21	\subsection{Limitations}
22	\label{sec:disc_limitations}
23	
24	Key limitations include the inability to distinguish contamination from legitimate capability change, the gap between our idealized injection model and real-world contamination mechanisms, and limited benchmark scope (MMLU + GSM8K). Full limitations appear in \Cref{app:limitations}.
25	
26	\subsection{Implications}
27	\label{sec:disc_implications}
28	
29	DIF-flagged items are not noise. They concentrate cross-generational discriminative information ($z = -8$ to $-49\sigma$ vs.\ random removal) and anti-correlate with the GSM1K contamination gap ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$), consistent with DIF items capturing genuine capability change that contamination does not confer. DIF should be understood as a diagnostic lens for understanding what changed across model generations, not a quality filter for removing bad items. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring alongside score reporting. DIF analysis provides a scalable, method-agnostic diagnostic that requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data.
30	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	We adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, a semi-synthetic validation framework, diagnostic checks, and statistical controls.
5	
6	\subsection{Data}
7	\label{sec:data}
8	
9	The primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.
10	
11	\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}
12	\label{sec:mhdif}
13	
14	In educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.
15	
16	The Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\citep{holland1988mh} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\theta$), where $K$ equals the number of distinct total scores observed in the combined sample, and computing a common odds ratio across strata. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \times 2$ contingency table:
17	
18	\begin{equation}
19	\label{eq:mh}
20	\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}} = \frac{\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \,/\, T_k}{\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \,/\, T_k}\,,
21	\end{equation}
22	
23	\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:
24	
25	\begin{equation}
26	\label{eq:delta}
27	\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \ln(\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}})\,.
28	\end{equation}
29	
30	The Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \leq |\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\%.
31	
32	The choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\emph{cross-subject matching} uses the total score on all subjects \emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks.
33	
34	\subsection{Cohort Designs}
35	\label{sec:cohorts}
36	
37	DIF requires defining a reference group and a focal group. We construct five cohort comparisons, each designed to isolate a different source of variation.
38	
39	\paragraph{Temporal.} The reference group consists of models released in 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) and the focal group consists of models released in 2024 ($N = 807$). This is the primary comparison for detecting items whose functioning changed across model generations.
40	
41	\paragraph{Ability-quintile.} Models within the same temporal window are divided by total score into quintiles. The focal group contains the lowest-scoring models (Q1--Q2) and the reference group contains the highest-scoring models (Q4--Q5). This comparison tests whether ability differences alone produce DIF, without temporal confounds.
42	
43	\paragraph{Within-family.} Llama-2 models ($N = 161$) serve as the reference group and Llama-3 models ($N = 308$) as the focal group. This comparison isolates generational changes within a single model family.
44	
45	\paragraph{Within-subject.} DIF is computed separately for each of the 57 MMLU subjects, with the matching variable (total score) restricted to items within the same subject. This design tests whether instability concentrates in specific knowledge domains.
46	
47	\paragraph{Random split.} The 2023 cohort is divided into two equal random halves ($N = 540$ each). This serves as a sanity check: no systematic DIF should appear between two random subsets of the same population (expected C\% $\approx 0$).
48	
49	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}
50	\label{sec:injection}
51	
52	To evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.
53	
54	\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = \mathrm{TPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{TPR}(\text{baseline})$ and $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = \mathrm{FPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{FPR}(\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation.
55	
56	\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this problem by computing the matching variable from all subjects \emph{other than} the one being tested. Under this protocol, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains, while detection power is preserved ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$ for four of five subjects). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for all semi-synthetic analyses.
57	
58	Diagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, instability decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \Cref{app:method_details}.

      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, a semi-synthetic validation framework, diagnostic checks, and statistical controls.\n\n\\subsection{Data}\n\\label{sec:data}\n\nThe primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.\n\n\\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}\n\\label{sec:mhdif}\n\nIn educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.\n\nThe Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\\citep{holland1988mh} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\\theta$), where $K$ equals the number of distinct total scores observed in the combined sample, and computing a common odds ratio across strata. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \\times 2$ contingency table:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mh}\n\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = \\frac{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \\,/\\, T_k}{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \\,/\\, T_k}\\,,\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:delta}\n\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}})\\,.\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%.\n\nThe choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching} uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks.\n\n\\subsection{Cohort Designs}\n\\label{sec:cohorts}\n\nDIF requires defining a reference group and a focal group. We construct five cohort comparisons, each designed to isolate a different source of variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal.} The reference group consists of models released in 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) and the focal group consists of models released in 2024 ($N = 807$). This is the primary comparison for detecting items whose functioning changed across model generations.\n\n\\paragraph{Ability-quintile.} Models within the same temporal window are divided by total score into quintiles. The focal group contains the lowest-scoring models (Q1--Q2) and the reference group contains the highest-scoring models (Q4--Q5). This comparison tests whether ability differences alone produce DIF, without temporal confounds.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-family.} Llama-2 models ($N = 161$) serve as the reference group and Llama-3 models ($N = 308$) as the focal group. This comparison isolates generational changes within a single model family.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-subject.} DIF is computed separately for each of the 57 MMLU subjects, with the matching variable (total score) restricted to items within the same subject. This design tests whether instability concentrates in specific knowledge domains.\n\n\\paragraph{Random split.} The 2023 cohort is divided into two equal random halves ($N = 540$ each). This serves as a sanity check: no systematic DIF should appear between two random subsets of the same population (expected C\\% $\\approx 0$).\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}\n\\label{sec:injection}\n\nTo evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.\n\n\\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this problem by computing the matching variable from all subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested. Under this protocol, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains, while detection power is preserved ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$ for four of five subjects). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for all semi-synthetic analyses.\n\nDiagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, instability decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.\n",
      "numLines": 59,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 59
    }
  }
}

```text
50	\label{sec:injection}
51	
52	To evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.
53	
54	\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = \mathrm{TPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{TPR}(\text{baseline})$ and $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = \mathrm{FPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{FPR}(\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation.
55	
56	\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this problem by computing the matching variable from all subjects \emph{other than} the one being tested. Under this protocol, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains, while detection power is preserved ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$ for four of five subjects). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for all semi-synthetic analyses.
57	
58	Diagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, instability decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \Cref{app:method_details}.
59	
```

  "tool_use_result": {

    "oldString": "Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework; diagnostic checks and statistical controls appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.",
    "newString": "Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework; diagnostic checks and statistical controls appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.\n\n\\subsection{Data}\n\\label{sec:data}\n\nThe primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.\n\n\\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}\n\\label{sec:mhdif}\n\nIn educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.\n\nThe Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\\citep{holland1988mh} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\\theta$), where $K$ equals the number of distinct total scores observed in the combined sample, and computing a common odds ratio across strata. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \\times 2$ contingency table:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mh}\n\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = \\frac{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \\,/\\, T_k}{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \\,/\\, T_k}\\,,\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:delta}\n\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}})\\,.\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%.\n\nThe choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching} uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks.\n\n\\subsection{Cohort Designs}\n\\label{sec:cohorts}\n\nDIF requires defining a reference group and a focal group. We construct five cohort comparisons, each designed to isolate a different source of variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal.} The reference group consists of models released in 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) and the focal group consists of models released in 2024 ($N = 807$). This is the primary comparison for detecting items whose functioning changed across model generations.\n\n\\paragraph{Ability-quintile.} Models within the same temporal window are divided by total score into quintiles. The focal group contains the lowest-scoring models (Q1--Q2) and the reference group contains the highest-scoring models (Q4--Q5). This comparison tests whether ability differences alone produce DIF, without temporal confounds.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-family.} Llama-2 models ($N = 161$) serve as the reference group and Llama-3 models ($N = 308$) as the focal group. This comparison isolates generational changes within a single model family.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-subject.} DIF is computed separately for each of the 57 MMLU subjects, with the matching variable (total score) restricted to items within the same subject. This design tests whether instability concentrates in specific knowledge domains.\n\n\\paragraph{Random split.} The 2023 cohort is divided into two equal random halves ($N = 540$ each). This serves as a sanity check: no systematic DIF should appear between two random subsets of the same population (expected C\\% $\\approx 0$).\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}\n\\label{sec:injection}\n\nTo evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.\n\n\\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this problem by computing the matching variable from all subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested. Under this protocol, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains, while detection power is preserved ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$ for four of five subjects). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol for all semi-synthetic analyses.\n\nDiagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, instability decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.\n",
